Three things fell out of holding that line. **A role whose default has to be another role over again is not a role** — a second, fainter `hair` was cut because no such token is universal (only `ua` declares a bare `-hair`; the rest scope theirs to one ground) and its default could only have been `line` again. **A surface with genuine extra needs declares its extras itself**, composed out of roles rather than added to them: `--rail-well` is `ink` at 10% and `--rail-spectrum` is a ramp of `fill`, and both stay in `Sidebar.tsx` where their one consumer is. And **the prefix belongs to the surface, not to the app** — one shared `--kit-*` namespace would read more tidily and be strictly more dangerous, because a page wrapper declaring it repaints every descendant, including a card belonging to a different faction than the page.

**A faction overrides by GROUND, and one faction does.** `sheet` is a content card; `chrome` is the app's own furniture wearing a faction. Seven of seven answer identically, because a faction's card sheet is normally the stock its chrome is made of. S.N.I.D.E.'s is not — its sheet is the photocopier slab pasted *on* a wall, pinned near-black in both cascades — so it names six roles on the `chrome` ground and inherits the other three (#2631, ADR-0085). **A faction that supplies nothing gets the card family whole**, which is why joining this vocabulary costs a tenth faction zero lines here, and **a viewer with no faction gets nothing declared at all**, which is how pixel-identity stays a property of the shape rather than a thing to re-measure.

**`fill` and `onFill` move together or not at all, and the type says so.** A hue is a fill and never an ink, so every faction declares the AA-measured ink that goes *on* its hue — and the moment a ground may re-point `fill`, an ink that cannot move with it is a contrast bug with no name to fix it at. The repo's one override proved the point before it shipped: S.N.I.D.E. chrome moved `fill` to the wall-end rung of its hue and left `onFill` on the sheet's near-black, a pair reading **2.07:1 in light** where the sheet's own pairing reads 6.93:1. Nothing rendered it, which is exactly why it had to be caught — a vocabulary nine lanes are written against must not hand the first lane to paint on a faction fill a broken pair. The `GroundOverride` union now makes a fill-only override unrepresentable; re-measuring `onFill` against an unchanged `fill` is still allowed, because only the ink going missing is the bug. The answer for S.N.I.D.E. is the wall itself: on that ground the fill is a rung of the wall's own ramp, so its paired ink is the stock knocked back out — **7.71:1 light, 11.36:1 dark**, and not a new measurement, because the contrast gate already reads the same two colours the other way round as the faction page's bare marker scrawl.

One hazard the shape creates and must pay for: a resolver builds its property names by interpolation, so `factionTokensDeclared.test.ts` — the guard for "looks tokenized, is not" — goes blind to them. It reconstructs the exact cross-product (prefixes actually passed × roles actually emitted) rather than loosening its regex, because "anything ending in a role name is declared" would exempt `--rial-ink` too, and that typo is the whole failure class. Note also what the gate does and does not see now: `factionContrast.test.ts` is HALF ENUMERATED since #2661 — 9 slugs × 2 grounds × 5 pairings × 2 cascades, 90 measurements generated by looping over this resolver — and half authored, so a pairing the role map creates is measured automatically while a pairing a SURFACE composes (a veil, a ramp, an ink on a ground the map does not name) is still unmeasured-but-green until a row is written by hand. Its docblock lists the five things the loop cannot see; read that before trusting the green.

**A LANE PINS ITS SLUG WHEN THE GROUND CANNOT MOVE (#2672, the na/Default lane).** The first surface migrated onto the map after the rail was the whole `Default*` family — twelve archetypes, 66 core-role reads — and it settled a question the rail never had to answer, because the rail's ground moves with its viewer and a card's does not. Every `Default*` archetype stands on something slug-blind: `factionSpectrumSheet()`, `factionSheet()`, `--faction-default-stamp-bg`, `.na-backdrop`. So the spread is `factionRoleVars(UNAFFILIATED_FACTION_SLUG, '<prefix>')` and NOT the subject's slug — an ink handed a faction while its paper stays na is the mistake that measured 1.03:1 on #2669, and `DefaultSelectCard`'s docblock had already ruled the same thing from the copy side ("`slug` picks the words and the mark and never a colour", the #796 / #418 / #636 bug three times over). **A pinned spread is not dead code, and what it buys is not a live theme: it is a NAME.** `SingularityPraxisDetail` dresses a Default-drawn subtree today by redeclaring `--faction-default-card-text` as a local, which repaints every na descendant in that subtree indiscriminately; `--praxis-detail-ink` hits one surface. The corollary for the lanes that follow: **a faction lane may hand the resolver a live slug only where the surface's GROUND is a role too.**

**A neutral is not a kit, and only a kit gets a lane.** The same issue's census counted 115 `--faction-default-*` reads across 28 files and about a third of them turned out not to belong to any faction at all — `EditCharacter` ("themed in the spectrum default skin for EVERYONE, regardless of the character's faction", #434), the onboarding cards a stranger sees before they have a faction, `MetataskPicker`'s multi-faction chrome, `StartHereMark` stamped over every faction's card, `proposeTask/factionSurfaces.ts` painting na paper under all nine hues. Those read na's tokens as the PLATFORM's neutral. A prefix there would be a name nothing can ever declare. **The test for a lane is not "does this file name my faction's tokens" but "is this file my faction's archetype"** — and the third group, travelling pieces like `DefaultPointsRing` and `DuelCard`, answers it from the other side: a piece that moves between hosts cannot name its host's prefix, which is why both already take their paint through props. All three groups are surface questions, and surfaces are columns, not rows.

### A faction's ornament is one primitive at named strengths (#849)

A faction's signature device is drawn **once**, as one parameterized component, and consumed everywhere. It is never re-drawn per surface — a hero with its own hand-tuned rosette and a join card with another is how a faction stops looking like one faction.

Two rules follow, and UA is the worked example:

**A mark is reserved, and reserving it is the point.** UA's ensō (`components/sigil/UaSigil.tsx`) is for the **score** and the **faction mark**. It is never a container border. A mark spent as decoration stops meaning anything; the restraint is the identity.

**A faction is BADGED with exactly one device, and it is the one `FactionSigil` hands out (#2217).** A faction may carry several devices — that is the paragraph below — but only one of them answers *whose is this*, and a player must not have to learn a second. Cozy Coven ran two: `FactionSigil` gave a **task** the witch hat while `CovenAvatar` badged a **member** with a crescent moon, and it was reported from the outside as exactly that ("unify on one set of symbols for both tasks and characters, rather than making players learn two"). Two things make the case reusable. **A keep-ruling defends the mark it was argued against, and nothing else** — #1209 kept the crescent on the reasoning that a *pentagram* would be mud at 12–16px, which was never a claim about the hat, and `CovenSigil`'s docstring says the opposite in writing (the silhouette is a brim and a leaning point, drawn to survive 15px). Re-read what an old ruling actually weighed before treating it as settling a new question. And **a device that encodes a MECHANIC is not a candidate for the badge, and is not displaced by one.** The crescent survives inside `CovenVote`'s phase plate, where five moons wax from new to full to build a 1–5 rating: a hat cannot wax, so unifying there would delete a control rather than a duplicate. Same split `covenSlip` still draws around `Spark`, the four-point sparkle ornament — ask what the drawing is *doing* on the surface, not which faction it belongs to. **The unification finishes by DELETION, not by a second dispatcher.** #2726 closed this case out: the badge Coven ran alongside the hat — `SigilMark`, a pentagram in a ringed disc — held six more mounts after the avatar swapped, and all six were handed to `CovenSigil` and the drawing deleted from the kit. The cost was named and taken rather than worked around: at the faction hero's 74px the hat is a flat silhouette where the badge was a struck, ringed object, and one mount wanting more setting is a follow-up for that mount, not grounds for keeping a second identity alive at the other five. A retired mark also leaves *false docblocks* behind it — `CovenSigil`'s own header still said the badge was live — so the sweep that retires a device has to grep the prose, not just the JSX. The ambient cost of getting this wrong, from the same report: a crescent is also the platform glyph for *do not disturb*, so a moon on an avatar reads as a **dormant state** before it reads as a livery. **A retirement is finished when the DRAWING is gone, not when the export is** — #2746 found four more copies of that same pentagram living in two files that never imported it (the feed frame's head charm, and the composer's stage mark, glyph scatter and masthead disc), invisible to #2726's mount list precisely because nothing imported the name it was hunting. The guard that holds is a tree-wide source scan for the *path*, and a scan scoped to the directory you were told to empty is the one that has quietly failed here before. **And a retired mark's MOTION goes with it, at the tempo of whatever takes its place.** The composer disc turned at 42s through `--ep-spin-dur`; what replaced it is `CovenCat` on `.cvn-wheel`, so it turns at 120s, the class's own, because a cat turning three times faster than every other cat in the kit is a second device wearing the same name. Retiring the mark that a rotation was argued *for* is not an argument against rotation — #2726 stopped the create-character hat turning because a hat has an up, never because the kit was giving up motion.

**Where an ornament may appear is part of its API, including where it may not.** `UaMandala` takes a `strength` of `full` (one surface — the vote control), `texture` (6–22%, behind page backdrop / faction hero / join card) or `absent`, which renders `null`. Dense, text-heavy surfaces — feed rows, comments, task lists, the editor — ask for `absent`. Encoding the third case as a strength rather than as "just don't use it" is deliberate: the ruling survives in the type instead of in a comment someone has to remember.

**That ruling is the mandala's, and it does not travel to the faction's other marks.** UA carries three devices — the mandala, the ensō and the lotus — and each has its own scope. `UaTaskCard` spent a release with no lotus because #851 read the kit's corner-bleed as something "the brief's strength ruling supersedes"; but the lotus is not radial concentric geometry, it is a ground wash, and `UaPraxisCard` (every bit as dense and text-heavy) had been floating one off its left edge the whole time. #1023 puts it back. When a rule names a component, check whether the thing in front of you *is* that component before you apply it — "UA ornament" is not one scope.

**"Absent on dense surfaces" protects the copy, so it stops where the copy stops (#2031).** The mandala is back on `UaTaskCard`, and it does not overturn the strength ruling above — it clarifies what that ruling is a rule *about*. `absent` exists so a pattern never lies under text read in bulk. A task card is two regions, not one: a reading column, and, below the rule the v3 kit draws to close it off (`data-cta-rule`), a sign-up region that carries no prose at all. Two mandalas flank the button there and nothing is read through them, while the column above stays clean and `uaDesktopSkin.test.tsx` asserts the two halves separately. The reusable move is the one #1023 already made from the other direction: **before applying a scope rule, ask which surface it was written to protect, then find that surface's actual edge** — "the task card" is a coarser unit than the ruling needs.

**The module is the enforcement, and it arrives late.** UA's devices were components from the start; WOW's were not — its wavy gold→plum rule and its googly-balloon bunch were hand-drawn inside `WowTaskCard` and again inside `WowTaskDetail`, byte-identical and invisible to each other. Nothing catches that: two copies both compile, both lint, and both look right. It surfaced only when a *third* surface wanted them (#1121, the praxis detail), which is the general shape — **duplication is discovered by the next consumer, not by the second one.** So the fix is the same one UA already models: one module per faction holding its whole vocabulary (`components/factionMarks/wowOrnament.tsx`, beside `components/duel/wowLists.tsx` and `components/factionMarks/wowMobile.tsx`), and the extraction happens when the third caller appears rather than being deferred again. Parameterize what genuinely differs and nothing else — WOW's bunch takes a size and a `bob` strength, because a page's live ornament bobs and forty of them in a flex-wrap card grid must not.

Where a mark ships as an inline SVG and where as a masked `public/` asset is a **weight** decision, not a style one — see `components/factionMarks/index.ts`. Both are token-tinted, so both follow the dark cascade. The same faction can legitimately carry two versions of one device at different sizes for different consumers; do not consolidate them on sight.

### Two grids of the same angle beat, and the replacement may not be a third grid (#2727)

Four surfaces paint WOW's 135° gilt hatch — the page backdrop, the mobile header, the profile band and the recruiting hero — and three of them agree on `22px 24px`. The hero was at `20px 22px`, laid directly over the wallpaper, and two pixels of pitch between two identical angles is a **moire**: the banner shimmered, and nothing else on the site did. **The tell for this class is that only one surface is wrong and every value on it looks reasonable.** A hatch is a legitimate faction ornament; a hatch over the same hatch is not, and no pitch repairs that. **Matching the pitch is the smaller fix and it is the wrong one for a surface that already sits on the wallpaper** — the mobile header and the profile band do match, and they read as *dense* rather than as shimmering, which is why they were left alone: the defect is the beat, not the doubling.

So the hero's layer was **deleted** rather than re-pitched, and the wallpaper reads through the frame. Two things about what may replace it.

**Nothing that repeats on a grid.** The failure was a repeating grid over a repeating grid, so a lattice of any pitch re-opens it. What went on instead is a scatter — rounded rects at drawn angles and a few discs — plus the faction's existing `BalloonBunch` at watermark strength. No new drawing was invented for the second half: the Court already had balloons on three surfaces, and §6's "one primitive" rule cuts both ways, forbidding a *new* device as firmly as a second copy of an old one.

**A scatter is SEEDED, and per-visit randomness is the rejected option, not the obvious one.** `seededRandom` (the house PRNG, `components/factionMarks/ephemeristsPlate.tsx`) draws it from the faction slug at module load, so it is byte-identical on every render and every reload. `EphemeristsNotationBand` already states half the reason — a device that redraws itself twitches whenever anything unrelated moves on the page, and a screenshot of it never reproduces. The banner adds the other half: a member meets this surface on every trip to their own faction page, and **a background that differs each visit reads as instability rather than as celebration.** It also makes a visual regression on the surface unassertable, which is the cost that is easy to miss when "a fresh throw each time" sounds like the more festive answer.

**A `bob={false}` is not the whole of "still".** The bunches opt out of their drift at the mount, but the googly pupils inside them ride `.wow-balloon-eye`, which no prop reaches. A watermark behind a wordmark that moves reads as a *rendering fault*, so the layer cancels the pupils by class — in `motion.ornament.css`, beside the rule it cancels, so the opt-out can never arrive a frame later than the animation. **When you still an ornament, grep the drawing for every class it hands out, not just the one the prop names.**

### A mark below the size of its own line is a different drawing (#1635)

The paragraph above allows two versions of a device at two sizes. This is the case where you owe one, and the threshold is arithmetic rather than taste.

Every faction sigil renders in `BadgedAvatar`'s membership badge, whose glyph is `badge - 5` px — **7px at the small avatar**, and 12–14px in a masthead datum row. The Ephemerists' kite is drawn on a 486×560 frame with a 26-unit stroke, so its rule is 2.9 rendered px at page scale and **0.6px at 13**. Sub-pixel geometry does not get finer, it gets grey: the crossed axes and four of the five discs stopped being detail and became a smudge, which is why "just scale it down" is the failure the reduced cut exists to prevent. So `EphemeristsSigil` draws the kite and the apex point alone below 20px, and 20 is not a round number — **it is where the design's own stroke crosses one device pixel** (20 × 26 / 560 = 0.93px), i.e. the mark is cut at the size where its line stops being drawable. Two floors do the rest: the rule and the point are each clamped to a device pixel, expressed once as user-units-per-rendered-pixel, so the small cut thickens rather than fades.

**A caller hands a mark one number, so the mark owns its ratio.** The kite is not square. Passing `size` through to both `width` and `height` would have stretched it on every mount, and asking each mount for two numbers puts the design's aspect ratio in eight call sites where it can drift. `size` is the **height** and the width follows the frame — which is what makes a masthead's 54×62, 38×44 and inline mounts one prop apart.

**And a prop whose every existing value became wrong is deleted, not reinterpreted.** The old mark took `stroke` in a 24-unit space, where `1.4` was right; in a 560-unit space it is a hairline, so every caller's number was wrong and none of them knew. Keeping the prop and quietly ignoring it is worse than removing it: the component derives a better weight from `size` than any call site can, and a knob nothing turns is scaffolding.

### A published ornament GROUND carries no opacity (#1635)

`--faction-ephemerists-grid` is the faction hero's ruled graticule, published so the surfaces queued behind it borrow it instead of each re-typing the crossed gradients. Two things about publishing a **ground** rather than a colour.

**The wash belongs to the mount.** The hero wears the grid at `.10` and a card at `.17`; an opacity baked into the image cannot be dialled back by whatever sits on it, and the second consumer then forks the token. Publish the marks, let the surface decide how far back they sit.

**A decorative ground goes in an ISOLATED negative layer, not on top with the content lifted over it.** The obvious shape — a `::before` at `z-index: 0` with the card's content lifted to `1` — needs a `> *` rule strong enough to lift *static* children, which means setting `position: relative`, which overrides any absolutely-positioned ornament the card already had. The plate skins are full of those. `isolation: isolate` on the host plus `z-index: -1` on the layer renders identically and asks the adopter for nothing: the host becomes a stacking context, so the negative layer paints after the host's own fill and before every descendant, and a card adopting `.eph-grid-ground` changes one class and nothing else. This is the same family as #1148 and #1255 — the question is never "may this element clip or stack?", it is "what is downstream of it?"

**Size it `100% 100% / no-repeat` even when that is a no-op.** It is, for a repeating gradient. It is exactly what a warped SVG needs, and it is the difference between swapping the warp in later as one declaration and revisiting every consumer.

### A faction's HEAD is a component, and the fifth copy is what proves it (#1634)

The Ephemerists drew one masthead — a winged sun disc over the faction's name in letterspaced Poiret One, centred on a night band — on four surfaces, in four copies that could not see each other: the praxis card, the praxis detail, the task detail and the composer. The task detail carried a *fifth* copy, of the disc itself, byte-identical to the shared kit's and invisible to it. §6's rule above says a device is drawn once; this is the same rule one level up, where what repeats is not a mark but **an arrangement of marks plus type**. `EphemeristsMasthead` is that arrangement, and the four skins now mount it.

**The second instance was Singularity's window lamps, and it shows what the copies drift into (#1979).** Three traffic-light dots on a terminal chrome bar, drawn on five surfaces — the praxis card, the task card, the praxis detail, the task detail and the feed frame — with a private `Lamp` declared in four of them and the fifth spreading a bare `ledStyle` onto three spans at a different size. What drifted was the PALETTE: four bars painted `--faction-singularity-led-red`/`-amber`/`-green` and the feed frame painted `-term-dim`/`-term-blue`/`-term-bright`, which is green, blue, green. Both trios are legitimate faction tokens, so no ratchet, no lint rule and no contrast test could see it, and each file rendered perfectly on its own — it was reported by eye, like #1634. **The consolidation that closes the seam takes NO props.** One exported `Lamp({ fill })` mounted three times by five callers is still five call sites owning the palette, and a sixth surface may still invent a trio; the kit owning the whole cluster — hues, wrapper and pitch — leaves nothing to pass and so nothing to disagree about. That is the shape to reach for whenever the repeated thing is an arrangement with no variation in it, and it is why the two ideas in this section's title are one idea: the copy is the drift.

**The third instance was ONE DOT, and being one dot is why it was skipped (#2092).** Singularity's process light — the small breathing tell-tale at the right end of the composer's window bar, beside the task card's in-progress count, opening the task detail's header row — sat three inches from the lamps through both of their consolidations and did not get one, because "an arrangement of marks" reads as *more than one mark* and this is a single span. It drifted identically: green on the composer, blue on the other two, both legitimate `-term-*` tokens, every file perfect on its own, reported by eye. **The extraction rule is per DEVICE, not per cluster** — the question is how many places draw this thing, and one span drawn three times is the same answer as three dots drawn six times. Two calibrations ride along. **Consolidating a device settles its whole pairing, not just the hue that was reported:** the composer's dot bloomed in `-term-halo-green`, coherent while it was green and wrong the moment the owner ruled blue, and the card and the detail bloomed not at all — a kit that takes no props has to *decide*, so the odd values die at consolidation. And **a value that cannot travel gets named at the mark rather than kept at the call site:** that dot ran the composer kit's `.ep-pulse` re-timed to 1.6s, and the mark now hangs on a task card, so it breathes on the faction's own `.sg-pulse` and the lost cadence is written into `SingularityProcessLight`'s docblock where reversing it would apply to all three surfaces at once.

**A census NAMES its surfaces; a count is what lets a wrong mount pass (#1998 → #2090).** The lamps' first guard asserted `toHaveLength(5)` over the mounts it found, and the sixth surface — the composer, with neither the expected identifier nor the expected token in it — was clean to *every* guard simultaneously: nothing to find in the import graph, nothing to find in the markup, and a count that five satisfied. #2090 replaced the number with an explicit list of six paths, and #2092's census is written the same way, so a new surface has to add its own line rather than quietly fit inside a total. Pair it with a rendered assertion at the surface that drew the wrong value, because the hue itself is only readable in markup.

Four things generalise off it, none of them Ephemerists-specific.

**A masthead's TWO SCALES are one prop, and everything else is a table.** `page` heads a page, `card` heads a sheet — and because #1635 made the sigil own its 486:560 ratio, the two scales differ by exactly one number per row. A `compact` boolean would have forked every internal (§1.2's rejected escape); a size *record* keyed by scale keeps them side by side where a typo in one is visible against the other.

**A band that held a fixed-height stack becomes a FLOOR when the stack starts sizing itself.** All four mounts pinned their band (110 / 84 / 108 / 84–68 px) around content whose height they knew. A masthead built from its own padding does not fit inside a number chosen for something else, so `height` becomes `minHeight` and the band's background register moves to `height: 100%`. Where the band is a *shared* block — `ComposerMasthead`, mounted by eight composers — the skin relaxes it through `style`, which is spread after `height`, rather than the shared block growing a second prop for one caller.

**A SHARED ANATOMY is a grid, not a flex row — and the task-card kit is the case that shows why (#2029).** Task cards v3 gives seven of the nine cards one masthead shape: the faction's mark hard left, the faction's title centred on the band. `components/taskCard/CardMasthead.tsx` owns that and nothing else — ground, rule, ink, height and the wordmark itself all arrive from the skin, in the `ComposerMasthead` shape above. The load-bearing detail is that the title is centred on the **band** and not on the space left over beside the mark: a flex row centres it in the remainder, so every card whose left cluster is a different width centres it somewhere else, and the drift is invisible until two cards sit side by side. A three-column grid with equal `1fr` gutters is centred by construction, whatever stands next to the mark — which is also what lets Singularity keep its window lamps on the bar without pushing its title off true. **The band belongs to the anatomy**: ornament that filled it or flanked its centre stands down (S.N.I.D.E.'s broken acid rule, Everymen's pair of cogs), ornament that only sits beside the mark stays, and a band with a *backdrop* (the Ephemerists' glyph registers, Coven's twinkle field) keeps it in a positioned box the skin wraps around the shared piece — so the shared piece never grows an ornament slot. Two cards carry no masthead at all, and that is a rule rather than a gap: `na` has no faction to name, and a band naming Albescent would un-hide it (ADR-0048).

**A shared piece travels only if it resolves its own look — and the bandless rule's one exception is about COPY, not symmetry (#2648, #2636).** Faction code shares along two axes and they are coupled: a piece can only reach a new surface if it paints itself from tokens without asking its host for a `style`. `factionBands` does, so the metatask seal mounts it and nine hand-lettered eyebrows are deleted — but the seal is the case that shows what "resolves its own look" does *not* cover, and the findings are worth more than the diff. **A band resolves its paint, not its SCALE**: every one arrives at card size (a `--text-title` wordmark, a 20px mark, Coven's pinned 44px, Everymen's form-factor padding) on an object a third the width, and the seal has no way to ask for less. **A band resolves its paint, not its INTERACTIVITY**: since #2167 it is unconditionally a `<Link>`, which is legal beside a card's links and illegal inside a control — the picker's row was a `<button>` wrapping the seal and had to become a wrapper with a transparent overlay control beside it. **And a band brings a router requirement with it**, which every host and every test harness inherits. On the bandless rule itself: the seal gets one because **its own copy has always named its issuer in plain sight**, so a band discloses nothing the sheet had not, which is not true of the task card or the praxis card and is why those two stay bandless. The two exceptional bands live in `metataskSeal/sealBands.tsx` rather than beside their seven siblings, so `factionBands.tsx` keeps saying "there is no `DefaultBand` and no `AlbescentBand` to import" — the strongest form the rule can take in a module. **The test for a third mount is that surface's own copy, never this precedent.** Coven's is the same shape read from the other end: the seal's near-white `#fff6fb` sheet needed a pink head, and mounting the band it already owned (`--faction-coven-mast` under `-mast-ink`, "a pink under white by day") was that ruling at no new paint — where tinting the shared `-ward-card` ground would have repainted six surfaces nobody had looked at.

**A shared piece can only give back what it prints — so check the COPY the hand-drawn thing carried before you delete it (#2648, owner ruling 2026-08-25).** The nine eyebrows the seal deleted were not faction labels. They were `praxis:detail.seal.label` = `"{{faction}} Metatask"`, and the issue that removed them described them as "the faction label" — so the word **Metatask** went with the name, and a band can only give the name back. The loss was real on two of the seal's four hosts: praxis *detail* sits the stack under a "Metatasks" heading and the composer says "+ Add a metatask", but the praxis **card** and the Tasks-page metatask list mount the seal with no surrounding heading at all, where a Coven band over a bare condition on a stranger's Ephemerists praxis reads as *this praxis is Coven's* — the exact misattribution the foreign-sticker contract exists to prevent. The fix is a caption key for **the noun alone** (`detail.seal.kind` = "Metatask"), never the old string restored: the band names the issuer now, and a seal that named it twice would be the doubling the band mount removed. Two things generalise. **A caption is not a heading** — it is a classifying line, so it takes each skin's existing eyebrow register (Coven's `CAPTION`, the plate's `SMALL_CAPS`, the terminal's `//` comment line, the zine's typewriter acid) at the label tier, quiet, in the body above the condition and never competing with the full-bleed band. And **an ink that moves keeps no coverage from where it used to be**: every one of the nine pairs was already on screen in that exact place before the deletion, and all nine are new rows in `factionContrast.test.ts` anyway, because the hand-authored half of that file is where an ink on a bespoke ground lives, and an ink it does not name is an ink nothing measures. Five of the nine do not stand on `--faction-{key}-card-bg` (UA's parchment ramp's darkest stop, Coven's ward panel, the Ephemerists' plate, Everymen's bill paper, WOW's chronicle sheet), which is why the block is written out rather than looped over slugs.

**A stylesheet hook into a shared primitive's internals is that same divergence wearing a different hat (#2056).** `CardMasthead` hardcoded its 20px mark and offered no size, so the one skin that draws a bigger one — S.N.I.D.E.'s brushed A at 24 — shipped `[data-masthead-mark="snide"] svg { width: 24px }` in `index.css`. It worked for a mechanical reason worth knowing: **a stylesheet beats an SVG's presentation attribute and loses to an inline style**, which is exactly why the drawing grew and the box did not. But it reaches past the component's API into markup the component owns, so it breaks silently the day `SnideSigil` stops being an `<svg>` — which UA's ensō already is not, being a CSS mask on a `<div>`. The rule the epic states for ornament states itself again here: **when a second skin wants the same thing the shared component will not give, the shared component grows the prop, once.** WOW flagged the identical gap in the same hour, which is the signal.

Two calibrations the fix turns on. **It is the DRAWING that grows, not the SLOT.** `markSize` moves the sigil and leaves the 20px box alone, because the box is what the `1fr auto 1fr` grid measures — a slot that followed the mark would take the centred wordmark, and Singularity's lamps, off true because one faction's mark got louder. A bigger drawing is centred in the fixed box and bleeds symmetrically into the band's own inset instead, which is what `SnideSigil` sets `overflow: visible` for; the ceiling is that inset, so roughly 24–26px, and a mark needing more room has to grow the slot *and* re-check the gutters, since a `1fr` track is floored at its own min-content. And **a hook like this is invisible to the harness that would otherwise pin it.** The repo's card tests read rendered markup, where a size living in `index.css` simply is not; moving it into a prop is what makes "S.N.I.D.E. draws at 24, everyone else at 20" a sentence a test can hold at all.

**A DATUM ROW is honest data or an honest egg, and never the thing in between.** The design hardcoded a plausible date and a plausible right ascension and declination *per surface*, which is decoration wearing the appearance of data — worse than either half. Owner ruling: the date is the surface's own (a praxis's submission, a task's filing), and the coordinates are **fixed**, pointing at one real place, derived rather than picked. Two consequences worth carrying. The derivation belongs in a comment at the constants, including the sign convention, or the next reader deletes two arbitrary numbers. And an egg is never labelled — a caption spends what noticing was supposed to buy. The corollary on the real half: a date formatter that renders a missing timestamp as the literal string `Invalid Date` puts an *invention* on the one row a player reads as data, so the guard belongs in the component rather than at each mount.

**Retiring a mark is finished when its LAST caller is gone, and the last caller is rarely on the list.** The issue named the winged sun disc through one class (`.eph-plate-crown`) on one surface. Four of the disc's five call sites were the mastheads it crowned and went with them; the fifth was a feed row's 14px band ornament, on nobody's list, and leaving it would have shipped a faction wearing two marks with no way for a reader to tell which was deliberate. Grep for the *component*, not for the symptom the issue named. The mirror of that: `WingedDiscSign` is a **different drawing** on a 24-unit square for the metatask seal, and a retirement stops at the edge of a drawing it is not about.

### A clip shaves the frame you gave it, so make the frame the GROUND (#1638)

`clip-path` cuts the element, and a `border` is painted at the element's edge — so a stepped, torn or notched silhouette eats its own rule along exactly the edges the silhouette was for. The Ephemerists' vote plate shipped that way: `stepClip(7)` chamfered two corners and took the brass border off both of them, which reads as a printing slip rather than as a cut. An *inset* box-shadow fails identically and for the same reason.

**Draw the frame as a ground and lay the surface a pixel inside it,** both clipped. The rule is then what shows through the inset, so the clip carries it instead of removing it. The inner chamfer's leg is one pixel *shorter*, not equal — an equal leg puts the two cut corners on top of each other and the frame opens again. (Strictly the parallel leg is `step − √2`; rounding down draws the chamfer's hairline slightly finer than the straights, which is a taper on a rule, not a gap.)

### When the marks become legible, the labels around them are scaffolding (#1638)

A vote plate is a scale, and a scale that has to be captioned is not one yet. The Ephemerists' metals ladder carried a dashed track threading the discs, a roman numeral struck on each, and an italic caption naming the hovered tier — three restatements of the row they sat on. All three went. Earn the removal first — a plate that drops its labels without making its marks carry the rank has only got quieter.

**But the mark that earns a removal may itself be too quiet to keep, and it is a separate judgement (#2142).** What #1638 offered in place of those labels was a **conic ring whose spoke pitch is set per tier**, pulsing around every reached disc: 60° at lead down to 22.5° at platinum, so the ornament densified up the ladder and rank was readable off it. The owner struck it a fortnight later, and the two reasons are both worth carrying. **Five of anything at once is fog** — the ring was drawn on every *reached* disc, so picking gold lit four haloes and the density that was supposed to read as rank read as noise. And **a difference has to survive its own size**: 60° against 45° is six spokes against eight, inside 44px, at the edge of a disc — a distinction that exists in the arithmetic and not on the screen. What actually carries the rank on that plate is the plain count of lit discs, which was always doing the work.

The pitch itself was not wrong, only permanent. It moved to the **cast burst** — the ~1.6s ornament the struck disc throws — where the same 60°→22.5° is the tick pitch of a ruled dial that turns as it opens, so casting platinum visibly throws a finer instrument than casting lead. Stated once, by one disc, at the moment it means something. That is the general shape: **a distinction too fine to hold as a resting state can still be legible as an event**, because an event has the viewer's attention and a resting state is competing with four of its own siblings.

**A decorative mark's accessible name is a different question from a decodable one's.** The kit's `GlossedGlyph` hides its English on `title`, and that is right *because* the English is a bonus already printed somewhere on screen: the tooltip is a convenience over a fact the reader can otherwise obtain. Where the glyph is the **only** carrier of its information, `title` is not an option — it is a pointer affordance, unreliably announced and unreachable on touch. That case takes a visually-hidden label (`sr-only`, already in the bundle). The test is one question, *is this word printed anywhere else on the surface?*, and it is worth asking out loud, because the two cases look identical in a design.

### Two designs drawing one ornament at two densities is not drift (#1654)

The Ephemerists' mark vocabulary lived in three files: the kit, plus a transcription in the task card and another in the task page — eleven local redefinitions, and a twelfth written out inline so no grep for a component *name* could see it. #849 above says why that happens; this is what to do when you find one, and the trap is in the second paragraph.

**Diff every copy against the kit's before deleting it, because a copy that has drifted is a live defect and the kit's version may be the stale one.** Here nothing had: all sixteen glyph paths were byte-identical in all three tables, and both membership differences traced to a shipped issue.

**But two numbers really did differ, and neither was wrong.** The card struck 40 cornice flutes and the page 52 — and the flutes are `flex: 1`, so the count *is* the fluting's pitch rather than a detail. Neither had drifted from the other: 40 came out of the task-card design and 52 out of the task-details design, months apart, and no shared source ever existed for them to diverge from. **A de-duplication that also picks a winner is a redesign wearing a refactor's clothes.** The count became a prop and both surfaces kept their drawing — which is #849's "parameterize what genuinely differs", with *genuinely* meaning traced to two designs rather than inferred from two numbers.

**Sweep the PATH STRINGS, not the component names.** A transcription is invisible to the import graph and invisible to markup — both copies render perfectly — so the only seam that can hold it is the source tree, and a name check misses a copy that has been renamed, inlined or split up. The `d` string cannot be smuggled: `ephemeristsPlateSurfaces.test.tsx` asserts that each of the kit's glyph paths appears in exactly **one** file. The corollary for a reviewer is that a green suite proves nothing here — 118 tests passed over the eleven copies and passed again over the imports.

### One carrier per object, and the carrier is a 3px border at full strength (#2519, epic #2496)

**Design-canvas rule, stated identically on three artboards, and it is the thing four surfaces got wrong at once.** A faction's spectrum is carried by exactly **one** mark per object, and that mark is a 3px spectrum border in `border-box` — the idiom `DefaultTaskCard` already ships. **Whatever bar, strip or hairline was doing that job comes off.** The four Albescent surfaces built from issue prose while the canvas was unvendored (#2504, #2505) each did the opposite: they joined the shared `.spectrum-frame::before` selector list, which defaults to `padding: 1px; opacity: 0.6`, and added *the faintest mark the kit can draw* **beside** the mark the drawing removes. The composer ended up carrying three spectrum marks where one was drawn, and the one we added was the dimmest of the three. Nothing rendered wrong; the page simply did not say what it was drawn to say.

**A shared ring's defaults are for HAIRLINES, so a carrier restates them.** `.spectrum-frame::before`'s `1px / 0.6` are the rail's and the feed card's, and a mount that "declares nothing of its own" is claiming to be a hairline. Two values — `padding: 3px; opacity: 1` — are the whole difference between a carrier and a hairline, and they belong in **one** rule shared by every carrier, because three of the five drifted to the wrong weight the moment they were allowed their own.

**The board's real border and the repo's masked ring are not interchangeable, and the choice is per-surface.** A masked overlay of a rounded rect and a real `border: 3px solid transparent` differ at the corners and in how they clip, and `border-image` does not clip to `border-radius` at all — which is why the mask idiom exists here. Three things decide it in Albescent's favour every time: the box belongs to **na** (growing `.faction-plate`'s 1px or a `Default*` inline border to 3px is a na edit, not a dresser's), a real border means writing `background-image` on a **descendant** of the `.alb-*` wrapper, which #2499's ground guard forbids outright, and the ring lands on the same pixels anyway — inset 0 with a 3px pad paints the outer 3px of the mount's own box. **Where the board and the repo differ is the technique, not the picture; say which you picked and why.**

**The cut that comes with it: the edge is for plates that hold TEXT.** A plate holding cards that carry their own 3px edge takes the **rule** instead — the same law as §5's "a bordered panel may not sit directly inside another bordered panel", with the spectrum making it loud. On the faction page that is three rings on five plates, and the two card-holding sections keep only their hairline.

**Taking the doubled mark off is a `display`, never a repaint.** The bar belongs to na and every unaffiliated player still sees it, so the dresser reaches it with two classes (`.alb-desk .spectrum-rule`) and hides it. A background written on a descendant is the one shape #2499's guard rules out, and `display` is the property that can do this from outside without touching a ground.

**Ask which mark is na's ALREADY before deciding which one comes off (#2553, #2559).** The rule above assumes the surface's carrier is the mark the dresser adds and the doubled mark is na's. Twice that was backwards, and both times because a second issue had moved na underneath the dresser in the same epic:

- **The composer.** #2520 gave na's sheet a `3px solid transparent` border with the ramp in the border box. `.alb-composer-edge` had been added on the premise that it replaced na's *masthead* band — which #2520 had already deleted — so the ring became a second 3px frame sitting one frame inside na's. **The survivor is na's border**, and `.alb-composer .ep-edge` went with the ring: it was hiding a band nothing had rendered for months.
- **The score stamp.** #2042 made the disc a `DefaultPointsRing`, a spectrum *ring*. The 2px band across the stamp's top edge, whose own comment called it "the rainbow's one structural appearance on this object", became the second. **The band comes off, on na and on Albescent** — the wrapper is two lines over `DefaultScoreStamp`, so one deletion fixes both. (#2634 then moved the surviving rule from *above* the working to *below* it, and the ring from the top of the plate to the bottom — the count is still one carrier and one rule, and the subtotal's new hairline is deliberately `-card-line` rather than a third spectrum.)

**A carrier that cannot cover na's border is not a carrier, it is a second frame — and the clip decides.** The masked ring works on the task card because it hangs off a wrapper *outside* the card, so `inset: 0` resolves to the card's border box and the ring lands on the same pixels as na's border. Mount the same ring through a **slot inside** a box with `overflow: hidden` — the composer's, which exists so no composer ornament reaches the viewport (#1028) — and the clip resolves at the **padding** box: `inset: -3px` is clipped away entirely and `inset: 0` is a frame inside a frame. There is no third option, so **the composer's carrier is na's static border and it does not travel.** That is consistent with ADR-0083 §3a (a padded ramp holding content is a *frame*, and frames stay still), and it is why "move the travel onto na's frame" is not a fix that can be written from inside the sheet. The upgrade path, if the surface is ever to move, is a slot rendering the ring as a **sibling of the sheet** inside the column that wraps it.

### One ornament per faction, ONE drawing of it, and it alternates (#2195, #2393)

**Owner ruling, 2026-08-17, and it overrules the entry above wherever the two meet.** Each faction has exactly one ornament — the card's BACKGROUND texture, never its frame, masthead, points mark or divider — and there is **one drawing of it, not one per surface**. A body on an ornamented page ground goes plain; a body on a plain ground wears it. Two supporting rulings travel with it: **"either burst, or plain"** (where the ornament is not worn the ground is *bare*, never a third texture), and the alternation binds **task cards and praxis cards**, which is where the owner said it binds.

**What #1654 keeps, and what it loses.** Its de-duplication method survives intact: diff every copy before deleting it, and sweep the source text rather than the component names. What it loses is its conclusion. #1654 parameterised a difference it had traced to two designs, on the reasoning that a de-duplication which picks a winner is a redesign wearing a refactor's clothes. The owner has now picked the winner deliberately — twice in the same ruling, once for Everymen's nine burst geometries and once for the praxis card's ruled paper, whose own comment cited #1654 as the argument for keeping it distinct. **A "two designs, months apart" provenance explains a divergence; it does not license one.** When the divergence is a faction's identity mark rather than a cornice's pitch, the answer is a decision, and the decision belongs to the owner.

**A survey of transcriptions under-counts, so build the guard, not the list.** #2195's inventory named seven Everymen bursts by file and line; `main` had **nine** — the composer ground and the faction page's member spotlight were both older than the survey and both missed, because a grep sees what it is pointed at. So the collapse ships with a scan that walks every `Everymen*` source and fails on any `repeating-conic-gradient`, comments stripped (a file that *records* the geometry it used to draw must not read as a relapse). A list is a measurement of one afternoon; a scan is the invariant.

**The GEOMETRY is what collapses; a REGISTER is not a second drawing.** Four of the ten mounts strike the burst on the union's red or ink plates, where the bill's paper ink is invisible. That is not another geometry — it is one ink, so the shared class exposes exactly one overridable custom property (`--em-burst-ray`) and a single modifier class supplies the knockout value. The stops, the mask and the origin are not overridable, because those are the drawing. **When a "one drawing" collapse hits a surface whose ground is a different colour, split the ink out and keep the geometry frozen** — the alternative is a per-surface copy that only differs by a colour and then, six months later, by more than a colour.

**The predicate belongs to the BACKDROP, not to nine cards.** Phase 1 (#2387) put a pattern-vs-wash table beside the backdrop components and hung `useGroundIsBusy()` off the context that already keys on the slug. A per-route flag was considered and is wrong even on the one route that survives the faction-page exemption: a Coven or UA profile paints a **wash**, which is not busy, so "the profile tells its cards to go plain" would strip the cat off a Coven card on a Coven profile. A backdrop that forgets to file itself shows up as an absent row, which is the safe answer.

**Faction pages are EXEMPT** (owner, 2026-08-18: *"we can have a busy faction background page with busy task and praxis cards on it"*). They claim it explicitly, through `useFactionBackdrop(slug, { cardsKeepOrnament: true })`, and the default is the law rather than the carve-out — a page that mounts a patterned backdrop and says nothing gets plain cards.

**Only the texture branches.** The frame, the masthead band, the points mark, the CTA, the dashed rules and the striped bars are identical on both sides of the predicate. That is testable without a DOM and it is worth testing: strip the ornament layer from the plain render and the busy render must be byte-identical. A "go plain" that starts taking chrome with it is how an ornament rule turns into a second card.

**And the byte win is smaller than it looks, because most of the copies were never on the critical path.** Nine geometries collapsed to one, three textures deleted, four tokens retired — and the blocking stylesheet moved only **−106 bytes gzipped** (22,994 → 22,888 at gzip level 9, the level the repo's own budget script uses; a default-level measurement here reports a different number and has stalled PRs before). Seven of the nine copies were inline styles in *lazy archetype chunks*, which the budget correctly does not count; the shared class is new weight in the one sheet that does. **Price a collapse by where the copies live, not by how many there are.**

### S.N.I.D.E. wears no tape, and it was SPECIFIED (#1708)

Owner ruling, dated 2026-08-14, and the reason it is written down is that every piece of evidence a later editor will find says the opposite. `tape` is a first-class token in **both** SNIDE palettes in the design kit, `ground()` draws two strips in the composer design, and epic #1179's seam table names it outright ("ground() — SNIDE grain + tape"). **A fidelity pass that restores it is undoing deliberate work, not fixing a regression.** Eleven strips came off nine SNIDE files and two shared ones, and `--faction-snide-tape`, `--snide-tape` and `.snide-tape` went with them; `frontend/src/__tests__/snideWearsNoTape.test.ts` is what keeps them gone.

**Deleting the class first is what makes the removal hold.** Leaving a declared token behind is an invitation: the next surface that wants a strip reaches for the name that already exists, and the metaphor is back on one file at a time with nothing failing. The two tokens here also held the *identical* rgba in two namespaces — latent drift that only ever got a chance to diverge because both were still declared.

**A strip can be carrying more than ornament, so check each removal site rather than sweeping the draw calls.** Ten of the eleven were absolutely positioned decoration and left nothing behind. The eleventh was not: the faction hero's eyebrow printed in the press's near-black *because* it sat on a tape ground, and that near-black is the wall it would have been dropped onto — an invisible line, not a plainer one. It takes the faction's xerox paper as ink instead, an existing token repointed rather than a new value. The same shape appears in the composer, where `ComposerGround`'s default negative inset exists so a layer can overhang the sheet: the strips were the only thing allowed to run off, so the ground stays pinned at `inset={0}` and the raster stays inside the stock.

**Not everything called tape is this tape.** SNIDE's task detail draws diagonal HAZARD stripes on its action plate, and its praxis detail hung "taped-up clippings" along a rail (black slabs since #2066, below — the point survives the repaint). Both are different visual families, both stay, and neither reaches for the retired name — which is why the guard needs no exceptions list. The test for the ruling is "does it look like a strip of tape", never "does the identifier contain the string".

### A clipping cut from the same stock as the wall stops reading as pasted ON it (#2066)

Owner ruling, 2026-08-17. S.N.I.D.E.'s two detail pages paste their blocks onto `.snd-detail-sheet`, the flyposted xerox wall. Those blocks were xerox stock themselves — `--faction-snide-note-paper`, which flips with the theme exactly as the wall does — so a "clipping" was the same colour as the thing it was stuck to. They are the faction's black slab now, `--faction-snide-card-*`, the ground its praxis card and its score plate already print on: **one black surface vocabulary per page**, instead of two grounds and a rule for choosing between them. It is deliberately *not* the wall recipe, which has the same problem from the other side.

**Pinning a ground without moving its ink is the trap, and it is invisible in review.** `--faction-snide-note-ink` is `#14110b` by day and `#f4f1e8` by night; pin the ground black in both themes and leave that ink on it and light mode gets black type on a black sheet — the WOW masthead's 1.08:1 (#2032) in another dress. So the move is a **pairing, not a repaint**: a block that goes onto the slab takes the whole `-card-*` family with it (bg / text / muted / accent), which is measured as a pair in both cascades, while the flipping family stays on the wall, where flipping is what makes it right. This is #1792's rule read forwards — the surface that does not flip needs the ink family that does not either, and the two families must not meet.

**Two things stop working on a black ground, and two things start.** Stop: a decorative strip of photocopier ink (`.snd-censor`) is invisible on it, so a section head that sits *inside* a slab takes the broken acid rule instead of the censor — which makes "which ground is this head on?" a question the code has to answer, not dress; and a translucent-black rule or raster (`-note-rule`, `-note-grain`) is swallowed whole, where `--faction-snide-slab-tooth` is the invariant cream equivalent the slab was built with. Start: acid carries **type** at 15.6:1, which paper never allowed it to do (the faction rule holds acid and pink as a ring, a rule or a fill on paper), and where it carries type it is spelt `--faction-snide-card-accent`; and the walked pink that was paper's emphasis ink falls to 3.1:1 on the slab, so pink retreats to the drawn line it already is everywhere else.

**A markdown body changes ground with the block around it.** `.markdown-preview a` reads the `--link-ink` seam, and unset, that seam is a light-cascade neutral measuring 2.19:1 on this slab. A block that carried the seam correctly *because* its ground flipped has to repoint it once the ground stops.

### …and the card goes ON the wall, because it has an EDGE the sheets did not (#2065)

Owner ruling, 2026-08-17, and read it together with #2066 above or it looks like the opposite decision. S.N.I.D.E.'s **task card** now prints the wall recipe as its own ground — two rasters and two washes over the `--faction-snide-wall`/`-wall-deep` ramp, xerox stock by day and pitch black by night. Its detail **sheets** were moved off that same recipe one issue earlier. Both moves are the same rule applied to two different objects: **a clipping reads as pasted onto a wall only when something draws the seam between them.**

**The card has that something and the sheets could not have it.** The card is a discrete object with its own outline, so a 1px edge (`--faction-snide-note-wall-edge`) and a printed offset shadow (`--faction-snide-note-wall-shadow`) mark where it stops. Those two are **load-bearing, not ornament**, and that is not a figure of speech: on the S.N.I.D.E. faction page `useFactionBackdrop` paints the page with the *same* wall, so the edge and the shadow are the *only* thing separating card from ground. Delete either as decoration and the card dissolves. The detail sheets are stacked blocks *inside* a page-wide wall with no outline of their own to draw, which is why they took the black slab instead.

**The light shadow has no blur, and that is the metaphor.** `3px 4px 0` is a printed offset — paper lying flat against a wall. A blurred drop shadow is a card *floating over* one, which is a different object. In dark it becomes a hairline plus a deep drop, because an offset is invisible against black and the hairline is what survives.

**A ground that is a RAMP makes contrast a position, not a pairing.** Every ink on this card reads against a different ground value depending on how far down the card it sits, and the manifest in `factionContrast.test.ts` has no notion of y. Measured at the sites the inks actually occupy, light mode runs 4.81:1 (the in-progress line, lowest ink on the ramp) to 14.30:1; the ramp's absolute foot would put the walked pink at 4.44:1, and nothing pink sits there. **When a surface swaps a flat ground for a ramp, re-measure at the position, and the fixture row is the floor rather than the reading.**

### A FORKED token family is invisible to every guard the repo has (#2321, #2322)

A faction's **directory tile** and its **task card** are the same faction drawn twice, and they are two mounts of ONE register — ground, inks, rules, faces, ornament. Not one markup: a task card and a faction pitch hold different content, so there is nothing structural to share and no component to extract. What they owe each other is the *palette and the type*, and when one of the two moves and the other does not, the result is a **forked family**.

**Both halves are real tokens, so nothing can see it.** S.N.I.D.E.'s task card had been on `--faction-snide-note-*` since #1023 — the family that FLIPS, because a clipping is a sheet of paper. Its directory tile was still on `--snide-ink` / `-paper` / `-acid` / `-pink`, four always-dark constants declared in the same stylesheet. Every lint passed. Every census counted them. `factionContrast.test.ts` measured *both* families, each against the ground its own documentation named, and both cleared. **A fork is only visible as a pairing in both cascades, or as the question "does this surface name a family its twin does not?"** — which is cheap, and is the shape the guard takes (`snideWearsTheClippingRegister.test.ts`, a source sweep against the task card *as rendered*: the card plus the atoms and the masthead band it mounts, because some of a register's inks are spelt in those and slicing the card alone reports them as inventions).

**The premise that lets a fork survive is usually written down somewhere as a fact.** `FactionSelectCard`'s docstring asserted *"the tiles are dark"* as a property of the grid. It was already false — Coven's tile is pink and UA's is cream — and it is exactly what let two dark-only registers look correct on a surface that flips: if the grid is dark, an always-dark palette is not a bug. **Delete the false premise in the same change, or the next reader restores the palette from it.**

**Gathering a register is nearly free to MEASURE, which is the argument for it.** Every ink the S.N.I.D.E. tile now paints with was already pinned in both themes by the task card's own fixture rows, because the task card is where they were measured. One row was new, and only because the tile's CTA hover names `--faction-snide-acid` where the card names `-note-cta-bg` — same hex today, and a fixture that measures a synonym stops measuring the site the day one of the two moves. **Invent an ink and you owe a measurement; gather one and you inherit it.**

**A retired name with no reader is a fork waiting to happen, because it still resolves.** The four `--snide-*` constants went out of `index.css` with their last consumer, the way `--gestalt-*` (#1661) and `--snide-tape` (#1708) did. The guard that keeps a family from coming back is not a ratio — it is the name being gone. Mind the second-order reader while you are there: `--font-faction-anton` was left unread by the same change, and `fontsLoaded.test.ts` treats an unread family token as live, because index.css is itself in the corpus the family check searches and the declaration therefore vouches for its own family.

**And a singleton's justification expires with its ground.** The tile's `1px solid #000` hairline was on the raw-colour allowlist with the reason *"the S.N.I.D.E. family has no rung under its ink"* — true of the family it was wearing. The family it wears now declares that edge per theme, because the card it was minted for has the same edge to draw. **A raw value parked as "no token exists for this" is worth re-asking at every ground change: the answer may already exist one family over, on the surface's own twin.**

**A register drifts without ever forking, and UA is that case (#2324).** Every colour the UA tile had ever named was a `--faction-ua-*` token, and all of them flip — no retired family, no always-dark constants, nothing a family-granular question would catch. What it did instead was **reach past the card's answers to lower-level pieces of the same kit**: the neutral `-rule` hairline where the leaf is bound in `-card-frame`, the bare `--faction-ua` fill where the card had already decided the button is a chip, a call-site `linear-gradient` where the kit names a three-stop stock. So the guard's question is per TOKEN, not per family — *does the tile name a token its task card does not?* — and the answer for a healthy tile is a strict subset. Two corollaries. **Two names for one value still have to be proven equal, not waived:** the leaf grounds on `--faction-ua-card-parchment`, inside a block index.css asks in writing not to widen, and the public `--faction-ua-parchment` is the same gradient — so the guard asserts the two *declarations* match rather than exempting the synonym. And **gathering the paint is not mounting the component**: the band's ground and rule came off `UaBand`, but `UaBand` itself draws `factionName()`, which #2332 had just lengthened to "Unwavering Artisans" — a tile with its own wordmark takes the paint and leaves the markup.

**A ground change re-opens the ink under it, in the direction that costs contrast.** Moving the UA tile's blurb to the leaf's `-card-muted` traded 6.73:1 for 5.13:1. Both clear AA for an 18px line, so it ships — but the reading to check is the *tightest stop the ink actually occupies*, and on this tile that is knowable geometrically: the crest band covers the ramp's top 44%, so nothing sits on `-lift` at all. **Ask where the ink is before asking what it measures**; a ramp the surface only partly exposes is a smaller measurement than the ramp.

**A fork also runs the other way: a LIVE family borrowed from another surface (#2325).** Coven's tile was never on a stale twin — its ground, inks, CTA and faces were the slip's already. What it had was a status chip spreading `covenSlip`'s `SLIP_PANEL` preset, whose background is `--faction-coven-ward-card`: the faction *detail page's* paper, correct and measured where it lives, boxed up and laid on a card. Nothing is wrong with the token; what is wrong is that it is a second **surface's** ground on a surface with its own. **A shared kit module's PRESET is how one surface's ground reaches another,** because a preset hides the family behind a name, and a preset with one reader is usually the reader's private decision wearing the kit's clothes — retire it with the reader.

**A token sweep is blind when a kit hands its paint over as JS constants.** The Coven tile spells no `--faction-` name at all; every colour arrives as an imported constant. A grep-for-`--` guard reports it clean in the broken state and the fixed one alike, which is the same class of blindness as the fork itself. **Resolve the module's bindings transitively before you ask the question** — `SLIP_PANEL` named no property itself, it reached one through `CARD`, and a masthead band keeps its face and its ornament ink in bindings *beside* its function rather than inside it. **And draw the register's edge deliberately: a drawn mark's INTERIOR is not the card's ground.** Coven's cauldron fills its belly with the same ward paper, so a sweep that walked into every module a card mounts would have pronounced the chip legal. The register is the ground, the inks on it, the rule, the faces and the masthead — not everything a card happens to contain.

**A fork can be invisible in one entire CASCADE, and Everymen is that case (#2327).** The tile printed on `--everymen-field` where the HELP WANTED BILL prints on `--everymen-paper` — two grounds, one faction, the sharpest split in the epic. And in light they are **the same hex**: `--everymen-field` is `#c1272d`, which is exactly `--everymen-red` and exactly `--faction-everymen-bill-mast`. Only at night does it diverge (`#3c1416` against `#e2433f` / `#c1272d`). So a reviewer sampling values in the cascade anyone looks at first sees a synonym and waves it through, and the *name* is the only question that separates them. **Read a token's declaration in both cascades before calling two names the same colour** — the collision may be the light half's accident rather than the token's meaning. (The light half's one real failure was the shape §5 already names: an `opacity: 0.9` folded into the status ink, 4.26:1 by day and 10.83 at night. An alpha on an ink is still the cheapest way to hide a defect in exactly one theme.)

**Where the register's answer is a drawing the repo has already ruled into a SHARED one that does not exist yet, go plain — do not transcribe.** The Everymen tile fanned a `repeating-conic-gradient`, and #2195 lists it as one of **seven** Everymen burst geometries that collapse to a single drawing, with "either burst, or plain — no third texture" as the accompanying ruling. Gathering the register would have meant copying the task card's copy of that geometry into an eighth place, five minutes before an open issue deletes seven of them. The tile ships with no burst instead, which satisfies the ruling, shrinks that issue's inventory by one, and leaves a mount that will want nothing but the class name. **A "gather it from the twin" instruction does not authorise a second transcription of something already scheduled for consolidation; the honest move is to take the plain half of the rule and say so.**

**The stray can be a single TOKEN inside a family both surfaces share, and then the twin's docstring is where the answer already is (#2328).** Warriors of Whimsy had no fork of any kind: the tile's ground, both inks and both faces were the decree's already, so every family-granular question passes. What it had was eight stray *names* — a synonym for the frame, a hand-rolled `0 20px 46px -22px` lift where the card declares a whole `--faction-wow-quest-shadow`, the avatar's gilt lozenge worn as a CTA, and a tag plate. **A twin often does not merely decline the alternative, it has ruled on it**: `WowTaskCard`'s docstring says in as many words that the design's gilt-button A/B prop "is not shipped … the choice it offers is already made". Read the twin's prose before picking a replacement; a register's disputes are usually settled in it, and re-deciding one silently reverses a ruling nobody will notice was reversed. **And a hand-rolled `box-shadow` is a stray in its own right even when its colour is legal paint** — a token holding a shadow *list* composes as the tail of a longer one, because `var()` substitutes textually, so a selected-state ring can sit in front of the card's own lift without forking it.

**#2325's borrowed plate has a mirror image: a plate minted in ANOTHER surface's block, for this one.** WOW's tag chip read `--faction-wow-chip-bg` / `-chip-border`, declared inside the faction *page* kit's block with the comment "the tag chip on the pledge card" — the page kit naming a token on the tile's behalf for a role the tile's own card had already answered with the crowned points plaque. The comment is what makes it look deliberate, and the tile was the two names' entire readership, so they went with the repaint. **A token declared in one surface's block and read only by another is a fork with a helpful label on it.**

**Gathering a register means inheriting its accepted compromises, not only its passing ratios.** The WOW chip's new plate is `-chronicle-panel` on the card's cream sheet at 1.12:1, inside a `-chronicle-gold` frame at 2.24:1 — below 3:1, and the owner's #2042 ruling is that it stays: deepening the parchment *closes* the frame's gap rather than opening it, and a plate dark enough to give the gold a real 3:1 needs relative luminance ≤ 0.091, which even the kit's plum (L 0.117, 2.54:1) does not reach. **When a gathered relationship is already at its practical ceiling, take it as it ships and cite the ruling** — a tile that "improves" it has quietly forked the register in the one direction a contrast sweep would applaud. (The chip's *edge* still gained: the retired `-chip-border` read 1.55:1 on the cream where the gold reads 2.24:1.)

### A surface that floats its own corner mark must SUPPRESS the card's (#1960)

A praxis card carries its own Task Crown, drawn by `ScoreStamp` in the score tag's corner whenever the praxis is top for its task. Three surfaces float a *second* fleur medallion over the same corner from outside the card — the six faction-page bodies, and the character profile's laurel over the character's highest-scoring praxis. The faction pages pass `showCrown={false}`; the profile did not, so on a praxis that was both top-for-task and the profile's best, two medallions stacked in one corner and the smaller one's rim escaped past the larger one's edge. It reads as one mark drawn wrong, which is what the bug report said, and it is really **two marks the reader has no way to tell apart** — the same failure as a faction wearing two heads (#1634), one level down.

**The rule is the mark, not the meaning.** The profile's laurel means "this character's best" and the card's crown means "best for this task"; they are different facts wearing one drawing, so they cannot both be shown at that size in that corner and only the outer one survives. `showCrown` is where that decision lives, and the question to ask at any mount that decorates a card's corner is *does the card already draw something here?* — the built-in mark is on by default, so a new surface inherits the collision silently.

### A SHARED SELECTOR THAT DOES NOT OWN ORDER GROWS ONE ORDER PER SKIN (#2634)

ADR-0049 split the score stamp on the logic/presentation seam and gave the shared half **row selection** — which of base / mult / meta / votes / habit a card may show — with the drawing left to each faction. That is the right seam and it was cut one notch too narrow. Selection alone left nine skins free to decide *sequence*, and they decided differently: three row orders, three subtotals on three different gates, and two copy registers for the same two terms. Four separate owner reports in one sitting — Coven's needless subtotal, inconsistent totals, the Ephemerists' spacing, na's total-on-top — turned out to be that one gap. **A shared selector is not a contract until it also fixes order; "which rows" and "in what sequence" are the same question asked twice, and only the first one was being answered.**

The canonical stamp, ruled 2026-08-24, is six lines and every faction draws all six in its own hand:

```
base  × mult      the multiplier is a CHIP on the base line, in all nine
──────────        the SUBTOTAL rule
  subtotal        base × mult, gated on `mult !== null` ALONE
+ meta
+ votes
+ habit
━━━━━━━━━━        the SEPARATING rule (ADR-0076), gated on `base !== null`
[the total mark]  last, on every skin
```

**Three things generalise past this surface.**

**A skin-local convenience becomes a defect the moment the formula behind it moves.** Coven, Everymen and UA each computed `base + meta` locally, and Coven's own docblock argued the field belonged nowhere else — *"a subtotal of two figures `scoreBreakdown` already returns … which is why `ScoreBreakdown` gains no `subtotal` field"*. That was true of the model ADR-0014 described. #2633 then moved the metatask **out** of the multiplier, and three correct copies became three wrong ones simultaneously, in three files nobody was looking at. The figure is `ScoreBreakdown.subtotal` now and no skin may contain `base + meta` or `base * mult` — which is the only shape in which "works in a different era" is true by construction rather than by nine copies agreeing.

**Two rules on one object need two names, or the wrong one gets moved.** Every stamp draws a *subtotal* rule (Coven's dashed keepsake line, Everymen's red strike, UA's plate hairline, WOW's flat gold, S.N.I.D.E.'s half-tear, the Ephemerists' brass, Singularity's half-strength phosphor, na's `-card-line`) and a *separating* rule between the whole working and the total mark (Snide's perforation, WOW's gold→plum bar, na's 2px spectrum). They look alike and they gate on different things — the subtotal on the multiplier, the separator on `base !== null` per ADR-0076 — so an issue that says "the rule" is ambiguous by construction. Name them at the mount.

**The chip is the operation, so it carries no word.** UA, S.N.I.D.E. and WOW already drew `×0.80` bare on the base line; the other six filed the multiplier as a labelled *row*, which read it as a fifth addend. Under the new formula `base × mult` on one line is arithmetically true, it is the shortest true drawing, and it leaves the subtotal's rule with **exactly one line above it** in every state — which is the invariant the whole order rests on. The Ephemerists' cell is the interesting case: its ruled chip spanned both columns of a figures-left/words-right grid (#2285), and moving it onto the base line kept that ruling intact rather than breaking it, because a ratio is a **number** and belongs in the number column. It gave up its `mult` label doing so, leaving `praxis.json:card.stamp.mult` with no reader — flagged rather than deleted, because an unread key is a copy decision.

**And a travelling piece still owns no outer spacing, even when an issue asks it to.** #2634 §5 asked `EphemeristsScoreStamp` for the top padding "the other eight have". The eight paddings it cites are each a plate's own **inner** inset, which that cell has too; what no stamp has is outer spacing, because #2655's law 09 forbids it and the crown's `top: -13` resolves against the root's padding box — so 8px there moves the ♛ off the Ephemerists praxis card's cavetto cornice, the exact sum #2360 pins. **When a spacing report names the travelling piece, measure the HOST first**: the gap belongs to whichever mount is short, and here the praxis card's masthead band sits directly above.

### A faction's TOTAL MARK is one drawing on both its surfaces, and an omitted element re-proportions it (#2019, #2034)

A faction shows a figure twice — the score stamp's total on a praxis card, and the points on its task card — and under ADR-0049 the mark is the device the figure sits *inside*, so it is one component in `components/factionMarks/` with `size` and its content as props. Everymen was the near-miss that names the failure mode: `PointsRoundel` had existed since #841 and the score stamp struck it, while the task card drew its own double circle a few pixels away in a different hand. Nothing was red — both looked like rubber stamps, and only a survey across all nine factions (#2042) could see that one of the two surfaces was not using the shared mark. **The tell is never a diff; it is asking, per faction, how many places draw the total.**

**A FACTION WITH NO DEVICE UNIFIES ON ITS GROUND INSTEAD, AND THAT IS THE RULE ARRIVING, NOT AN EXEMPTION FROM IT (#2042, owner ruling 2026-08-18).** Eight of the nine share a drawing — cauldron, compass rose, roundel, ensō, pen circle, lit well, spectrum ring. WOW has nothing to extract: both its treatments are **typographic plaques**, a numeral set in the medieval face inside a 2px gold frame, and there is no `factionMarks/` component in a numeral. Asking "how many places draw the total" returned *two* for WOW and stayed at two for nine months, because the survey was looking for a device. What was genuinely unshared was the **ground**: `--faction-wow-stamp-bg` was declared `var(--faction-wow-chronicle-panel)` — the card plaque's own **fill** — so the score stamp stood on the one colour in the register that a WOW plaque is defined *against*. The plaque had no ground (1.00:1), the gold frame fell to 2.00:1 in light, and the stamp's quiet ink sat at **4.24:1, under AA**, unseen because `factionContrast` has never named that token as a surface. The repoint to `--faction-wow-chronicle-bg` — the cream sheet the decree's plaque has always been struck into — is the entire unification, and every ink on the plate gains in both themes. **So the per-faction question is not only "how many components draw the total" but "how many GROUNDS does the total stand on".** A token aliasing a sibling surface's fill is the shape to look for; the two treatments' devices may still differ, and here they must (#2070 took the `✦` off the card, #840 carves it out on the stamp).

**A mount that omits an element does not just lose it — it inherits room, and the proportions have to answer.** The roundel's arced legend reads "★ VERIFIED ★ ON THE RECORD", a claim only a *scored* praxis can make, so a task card passes no `arcLabel`. That leaves two texts in a ring tuned for three, and the unit caption keeps a size chosen to make space for a legend that is not there — at the task card's 70px seal, sub-6px mush. The caption grows when the legend is absent (`7.5` → `10.5` in the drawing's own user space), which is the *component* owning its geometry rather than the caller nudging a prop. The praxis mount passes a legend and is untouched, so one drawing still holds both. Optionality on a shared mark is a proportion decision, not just a `?`.

**Ornament for a LAZY archetype belongs in the `.tsx`, and this is measured, not stylistic.** The nine task cards are lazy (`factions/*.ts` → `lazyArchetype`), so `EverymenTaskCard` ships in its own 3.7 KB chunk the initial load never fetches — bytes drawn there cost the budget nothing. A rule in `index.css` lands in the one blocking stylesheet, which sits ~1 KB under its FAIL line.
